**Part B-2 — flags**

- ⚠ **Internal date mismatches printed in the table:** "1939 — World War II began _(September 1)_" here vs "Second World War broke out in Europe on Sep. 3, 1939" in the Congress-Ministries paragraph (Part B above); "1911 — Delhi Darbar … Delhi becomes the capital"; "1962" appears twice ("India lost brief border war with China" and "Sino-Indian war"); "1839: Death of Maharaja Ranjit Singh" (while "1780 Birth of Maharaja Ranjit Singh"). Left as printed.
- ⚠ **Row wording garbled or misspelt in print** ("Taskent", "Minsiter", "Sriperumbdur", "Kapil Dev … kapil Dev") — kept verbatim.
- ⚠ Scan legibility: p.83 right column top ("1948 … C. Rajagopalachari became the first and last Indian Governor-General …") and p.84 rows read at 120 dpi; individual digits look clear but were not zoom-verified; **the 1981–1990 award/prize rows in particular are outside Modern History proper and were not cross-checked.**
- The **Ancient and Medieval bands** of this same table (book p.82 left column and right column down to "1739 Nadir Shah invaded India") are **not** extracted in this file.

**PART C — Cross-book notes / discrepancies (books only — no test cross-referencing)**

**C0. How the Gandhian material is split across files (a filing decision, not a book claim)**

- **Lucent** prints the whole Gandhian Era (1917–47) as **one continuous section** (book pp.76–82) — Gandhi chronology, Gandhi facts, Rowlatt … Independence Act, integration of states. It is transcribed once, in full, in **Part B above**.
- **Parmar** splits the same period across ch.6 (this file, Part A: Gandhi biography, early Gandhian movements, GoI Act 1919, Rowlatt, Jallianwala, Khilafat, Non-Cooperation, Swaraj Party, Belgaum 1924), ch.7 (Civil Disobedience Movement & Simon Commission), ch.8 (Quit India Movement, incl. Communal Award / GoI Act 1935 / August Offer / Cripps / INA / Cabinet Mission / Mountbatten — as its map shows) and ch.9 (Governor-General & Viceroy). Those chapters are in their own files (civil_disobedience_and_simon_commission_raw_extraction.md, quit_india_and_independence_raw_extraction.md, governors_general_and_viceroys_raw_extraction.md); each compares itself against **Lucent Part B of this file** rather than re-transcribing it.
- Below, Part C covers only the **Parmar ch.6 ⇄ Lucent** overlap (Gandhi's life, pre-1920 Gandhian campaigns, Rowlatt/Jallianwala, Khilafat, Non-Cooperation, Swaraj Party, GoI Act 1919).

**C2. Champaran, Ahmedabad, Kheda (1917–18)**

- **Champaran (1917).** Both: Raj Kumar Shukla invited Gandhi; both label it Gandhi's **first Civil Disobedience Movement in India**. Parmar adds Tinkathia system (indigo on 3/20th of land), co-workers (Rajendra Prasad, Mazhar-ul-Haq, J.B. Kriplani) and the **Champaran Agrarian Act (1918)** (abolished Tinkathia; suspended Abwab tax). Lucent adds April 1917, "Indigo planters of Bihar", and the Lucknow-session meeting of Shukla and Gandhi (Dec 1916).
- **Ahmedabad (1918).** Both: **first hunger strike** as a weapon. Parmar: invited by Anusuya Sarabhai; mill owners discontinued plague bonus; workers demanded 50%, owners offered 20%, settled at 35%. Lucent: Feb. 1918; industrial workers; no invitation, bonus, or percentage detail.
- **Kheda (1918).** Both: first Non-Cooperation Movement; failure of crops → rent/land tax difficulty. Parmar adds result (tax waived for the year and next; confiscated property returned; increased rate reduced). Lucent: March 1918, Gujarat peasants; no result given.
- Lucent also gives the Aug 1917 policy statement — **Montagu Declaration / August Declaration of 1917** — at the top of book p.76 (see the Part B addendum in bengal_partition_swadeshi_extremists_raw_extraction.md); Parmar has no separate August Declaration entry in ch.6.

**C3. Government of India Act 1919 (Montagu-Chelmsford Reforms)**

- **Parmar** (p.174): dedicated section — Montagu (Secretary of State) and Chelmsford (Viceroy); separate electorate extended to Anglo-Indians, Christians; **dyarchy in provinces** (Reserved: Land revenue, Police, Finance — under the Governor with Executive Council; Transferred: Agriculture, Health, Education — under Ministers from Legislative Council); **bicameral legislature at the Centre**.
- **Lucent**: no dedicated section in the pages read. It mentions "dyarchy system introduced by the Montague-Chelmsford Reforms of 1919" (Simon Commission paragraph, p.78), "legislative councils (established under Mont-ford Reforms of 1919)" (Swaraj Party paragraph, p.78), and the date row "1919 Montague-Chelmsford Reforms introduced" (Misc Dates). No provisions, no reserved/transferred list. Lucent's spellings: "Montague-Chelmsford", "Mont-ford".

**C5. Khilafat Movement**

- **Parmar** (pp.174–175): heading "(1919)"; launched after WWI when Britishers removed the Khalifa of Turkey from power; **All India Khilafat Committee** set up in Bombay, March 1919 (Ali Brothers, Maulana Abul Kalam Azad, Hasrat Mohani, Ajmal Khan); **All India Khilafat Conference, Delhi, Nov 1919**, headed by Gandhi (first President); "opposed by Congress and B G Tilak"; box: Indian National Liberation Federation — Surendranath Banerjee.
- **Lucent** (pp.77–78): heading "Khilafat movement (1920-22)"; the Caliph/Sultan of Turkey as religious head; WWI; Ali Brothers (Mohammad Ali and Shaukat Ali) launched movement in 1920; Maulana Abul Kalam Azad also led; supported by Gandhi and INC. The chronology row (p.77): "The All India Khilafat Conference elected Gandhi as its president (Nov. 1919, Delhi)".
- ⚠ **Movement date:** Parmar heading 1919; Lucent heading 1920–22 (but Lucent's own chronology row records the Nov 1919 Delhi conference). ⚠ **Congress stance:** Parmar says the Nov 1919 Conference "was opposed by Congress and B G Tilak"; Lucent says the movement "was supported by Gandhiji and INC" (in reference to the 1920 movement). Not reconciled by either book.
- Parmar-only: Hasrat Mohani, Ajmal Khan names, March 1919 Bombay Committee, INLF box. Lucent-only: Caliph-as-religious-head explanation; "paved the way for Hindu-Muslim unity".

**PART A — Parmar's SSC Fatman (2nd Ed., 2026): "BENGAL PARTITION" (book pp.169–172, PDF 185–188)**

Chapter closes with a "■■■" end marker at the bottom of the left column of book p.172 (confirmed by direct read; the right column of p.172 is blank). Parmar's chapter map (PDF 170) lists this chapter's sub-headings as: Bengal Partition: 1905 · Sessions of Congress · Moderates and Extremists · Revolutionary Activities · India Councils Act, 1909 · Home Rule League (HRL), 1916 · Lucknow Session, 1916.

**\[book p.169 / PDF 185\]**

**BENGAL PARTITION: 1905**

- _Lord Curzon (1899-1905),_ the then Viceroy of India, proposed the partition in _1903._
- The partition created a divide between _Hindus and Muslims,_ leading to communal tensions that would echo throughout India's struggle for independence.

\[Map graphic: "Bengal 1905–1911 — Bengal constituted _1/4th_ of the total British India population." with a small comparison table (verified on a zoomed re-render):\]

|                 | **Bengal (1902-'09)** | **Eastern-Bengal & Assam (1902-'09)** |
| --------------- | --------------------- | ------------------------------------- |
| Area (Km²)      | 366,692               | 275,938                               |
| Population (Mn) | 54                    | 31                                    |
| Muslims (Mn)    | 9                     | 18                                    |
| Muslims (%)     | 16.67                 | 58.06                                 |

_Revolutionary Policies of Lord Curzon_

1. _Calcutta Corporation Act (1899):_ Reduced the number of elected Indian members in the corporation.
2. _Official Secrets Act (1904):_ Enacted to curb the freedom of the Press.
3. _Indian Universities Act (1904):_ Increased government control over universities.
4. _Bengal Partition, 1905_

_Bengal was divided into:_

1. East Bengal (Muslim) → _At present:_ Assam and Bangladesh.
2. West Bengal (Hindu) → _At present:_ West Bengal, Odisha, and Bihar.

_Reasons given by Britishers for Partition of Bengal_

- The British justified the partition of Bengal in 1905 primarily on grounds of _administrative convenience_ and the need to improve governance in a region with a large and diverse population.
- The actual reason was to _"Weaken the Bengal"_ (the nerve centre of Nationalist activities).
- _Partition announced on: July, 1905._
- _Partition came into Force: 16th October 1905._
- _Reaction:_ Observed as a _"Day of Mourning"._

**SESSIONS OF CONGRESS**

_1905 Session of Congress_

- The Session happened in Benaras and was presided over by _G. K. Gokhale._
- It approved _Anti Partition Movement/Swadeshi Movement._ The formal proclamation of the Swadeshi Movement (foreign made goods and products were to be boycotted) was made on _7th August 1905_ in _Calcutta Townhall._

_(right column)_

_1906 Calcutta Session of Congress_

- It was presided over by _Dadabhai Naoroji._

_Four Important Resolutions were Taken in this Session:_

- _Swaraj_ as a goal adopted.
- _Boycott_ of foreign goods.
- _Swadeshi._
- _National Education_ (formation of National Education Council).

_1907 Surat Session of Congress_

- It was presided by _Rash Behari Ghosh._
- In this Session, the Congress split into Moderates and Extremists.

_Reasons for their Separation_

- The _role of the INC in the manner of opposing the Partition of Bengal_ was the main cause of the Split.
- The Extremists wanted the agitation to be a mass struggle all over the country. But the Moderates still had faith in the British administration and wanted to confine the boycott to Bengal and foreign goods only.
- Britishers adopted _"Carrot and Stick Model"_ within Congress. _Carrot:_ Appeasement to Moderates; _Stick:_ Suppressing Extremists.

**MODERATES AND EXTREMIST**

- Methods adopted by Moderates were: Petitions, newspapers, pamphlets.

_Moderates_

- SN Banerjee, KK Mitra, GK Gokhale, Pherozshah Mehta, etc.

_Extremists_

- Lala Lajpat Rai, Bal Gangadhar Tilak, Bipin Chandra Pal, Aurobindo Ghosh.

\[Box\] _Bande Matram journal was written by: Bipin Chandra Pal._

_Methods Adopted by Extremists — According to Extremists:_

- They had hatred for foreign rule; since no hope could be derived from it.
- _Swaraj to be the goal_ of national movement.
- Direct political action was required.
- Belief in capacity of masses to challenge the authority.
- Personal sacrifices required and a true nationalist to be always ready for it.

**\[book p.170 / PDF 186\]**

\[Box, continuation of the Extremists' methods\]

- Formation of _Swadesh Bandabh Samiti_ by _Ashwini Kumar Dutta_ at _Barisal_ (in Bangladesh now).
- National Educational Centres to be formed.
- Indigenous Enterprise: _Swadeshi Steam Navigation Company_ by _V.O. Chidambaram Pillai_ in Tamil Nadu.

_Bal Gangadhar Tilak_

- He organised _Ganpati, Shivaji_ festivals to attract and mobilise the youth (in Maharashtra).
- He was titled as _"Father of Indian Unrest"_ By _Valentine Chirol_ in his book _"India Unrest"._
- He is famously known as _"Lokmanya Tilak"._
- Newspapers Published: — _Mahratta_ (in English) — _Kesari_ (in Marathi)
- \[Image: B.G. Tilak\]

_Newspapers during Anti-partition Movements_

- _Hitabadi_ by: Dwijendranath Tagore.
- _Sanjibani_ by: Krishna Kumar Mitra.
- _Bengalee_ by: Surendranath Banerjee.
- B.G. Tilak said that: _Freedom is my Birth Right and I Shall have it._
- He was sent to _Mandalay Jail_ (Burma) from _1908 to 1914._
- During his imprisonment in Mandalay Jail, he wrote the famous work _Gita Rahasya._

_Abanindranath Tagore_

- He painted the image of _Bharat Mata_ in _1905._ The painting was a symbol of nationalism during the Indian independence movement.
- He was the principal artist of _Indian School of Oriental Art (1907)._

_Actions to Show Unity_

- People tied Rakhi to each other to show unity.
- _Rabindranath Tagore_ wrote a song: _Amar Sonar Bangla_ (now it is National Anthem of Bangladesh).
- _Sudesa Geethangal,_ a collection of poems was written by _Subramaniya Bharti._
- People sang _Vande Matram_ (National Song); however Government banned it.
- \[Image: "The painting of Bharat Mata"\]

_Leaders_

- Poona and Bombay: Bal Gangadhar Tilak
- Delhi: Sayed Haider Raza
- Madras: Chidambaram Pillai
- Punjab: Lala Lajpat Rai

_(right column)_

_Response of Muslims_

- Muslims did not support Anti-Partition Movement.
- Formation of _All-India Muslim League_ on _30th Dec 1906_ in _Dhaka_ (now Bangladesh) by _Nawab Khwaja Salimullah_ and _Aga Khan._

**INDIAN COUNCIL ACT, 1909**

- The Indian Council Act of 1909 is also known as _Morley-Minto Reform. Lord Morley was the then Secretary of State and Lord Minto was the then Viceroy of India._
- The provision for separate electorate for Muslim was introduced by _Lord Minto_ (he is also known as _"Father of Communal Electorate"_).
- Viceroy Executive Council will have one Indian → _First Indian_ to be a part of it was _Satyendra Prasad Sinha._

_Annulment of Partition_

- In _1911, Lord Hardinge II_ organised _Delhi Durbar (3rd Delhi Durbar)_ for the coronation of _King George V._

_Key decisions:_

1. Reunification of Bengal, announced by _King George V._
2. The capital was shifted from _Calcutta to Delhi in 1911._

_Note:_ → Delhi Durbans have been organised three times :

- _1st Delhi Durbar (1877):_ Held under _Lord Lytton_ to proclaim Queen Victoria as Empress of India.
- _2nd Delhi Durbar (1903):_ Held under _Lord Curzon_ to celebrate the coronation of Edward VII.
- _3rd Delhi Durbar (1911):_ Held under _Lord Hardinge II;_ during this, the Partition of Bengal was annulled and the capital was shifted from Calcutta to Delhi.

**REVOLUTIONARY ACTIVITIES**

- _1890:_ Shivaji and Ganpati festivals organised by _Bal Gangadhar Tilak._
- _1897:_ Chapekar Brothers (Vasudeo, Damodar and Balkrishna) assassinated _W.C. Rand_ (Plague Commissioner of Poona).
- _1899:_ Mitra Mela was founded by Savarkar Brothers in Poona.
- _1902:_ Anushilan Samiti (Bengal) Founded by _Satish Chandra Basu, Jatindranath Banerjee_ and _Pramathanath Mitra;_ later _Barindra Kumar Ghosh_ formed the _Jugantar Group (1906)._ And Bihar branch (Patna) established by _Sachindra Nath Sanyal._
- _1904:_ Mitra Mela was merged with _Abhinav Bharat Society_ by _V. D. Savarkar._

**\[book p.171 / PDF 187\]**

- _1905:_ Indian Home Rule and Indian House was formed by _Shyamji Krishna Verma_ in London.
  - Journal written: _The Indian Sociologist._
- _1907:_ _Madam Bhikaji Cama_ became the _first Indian to hoist Indian Flag on foreign soil (in Stuttgart, Germany)._ She published journal _Vande Mataram._
  - The flag hoisted by Madam Bhikaji was smuggled into British India by _Indulal Yagnik._
- _1908:_ (Alipore/Manicktala Bomb Conspiracy):
  - Attempt to murder _Judge Kingsford_ in Muzaffarpur.
  - _Khudiram Bose_ and _Prafulla Chaki_ were involved in this.
  - The bomb accidentally killed two innocent women (Mrs. Kennedy and her daughter) instead of Kingsford.
  - Result: Prafulla Chaki shot himself to avoid arrest after the Muzaffarpur incident (1908); Khudiram Bose was hanged in _Muzaffarpur jail._
  - _Kanailal Dutta_ killed the witness _Narendranath Goswami._
- _1909:_ _Madan Lal Dhingra_ killed _Curzon Wyllie._
- _1909:_ AMT Jackson was killed by _Anant Lakshman._
- _1910:_ Swadesh Sevak Home (Vancouver), United India House (in USA) was founded by _Taraknath Das_ and _G.D. Kumar._
- _1913:_ _Ghadar Party_ was formed in San Francisco (HQ: USA) by:
  - _Lala Hardayal, Sohan Singh Bhakna (First President of Ghadar Party), Barkatullah, Parmanand, Kartar Singh._
- Note: _Ram Nath Puri_ succeeded _Lala Hardayal_ (Founder/First Secretary) as the Secretary of the Ghadar Party.
- _1915:_ Berlin Committee was formed by _Virendranath Chattopadhyay._
- \[Box\] _In 1911, V.D. Savarkar was sent to Cellular Jail in Andaman & Nicobar Islands._

_Komagata Maru Incident, 1914_

- A _Japanese ship_ taken on lease by Sikh businessman and sailed to Vancouver (Canada) from Hong Kong.
- This incident involved a ship carrying _376 Indian passengers, primarily Sikhs_ who were denied entry to Canada due to discriminatory immigration laws. Forced to return to India, they faced further mistreatment, highlighting the struggle against racial discrimination and colonial oppression.

_Defence of India Act 1915_

- This Act was enacted by the British government in India to _suppress the Ghadrites particularly in Punjab,_ during World War I.
- This Act was later replaced by a Permanent Act called _Rowlatt Act._

_(right column)_

_World War I_

- In _1914, World War I began_ → It was fought between the _Central Powers and the Allied Powers._

_Allies of World War I (they won the war)_

- Belgium, France, Great Britain (and Commonwealth), Italy, Japan, Russia, and USA.

_The Central Power_

- Austria-Hungary, Bulgaria, Germany, and Ottoman Empire (Turkey).

_During the World War I, in the context of the Indian nationalist:_

- The Moderates supported the war as a _matter of duty._
- The Extremists supported the war, hoping for future _self governance_ in return.
- While the Revolutionaries, saw the war as an _opportunity to actively fight against_ British rule and seek immediate Independence through forceful means.

**HOME RULE LEAGUE (HRL), 1916**

- _Two Home Rule Leagues were formed by:_ Bal Gangadhar Tilak and Annie Besant, both in _1916._
- After B G Tilak's death in _1920,_ his Home Rule League was renamed as _Swarajya Sabha by Mahatma Gandhi._

| **Bal Gangadhar Tilak's HRL**                                                       | **Annie Besant's HRL**           |
| ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------- |
| • HQ: Poona                                                                         | • HQ: Madras                     |
| • It was founded first.                                                             | • It had more branches of HRL.   |
| • Centre: Maharashtra _(excluding Bombay City),_ Karnataka, Central Province, Berar | • Centre: Madras and Bombay City |

**LUCKNOW SESSION, 1916**

- This session was presided over by _Ambika Charan Mazumdar._
- _In this session:_
  1. Reunion of Extremists and Moderates.
  2. _Lucknow Pact_ (a Joint Pact) was signed between _INC_ and _Muslim League._

_Demands of INC_

- Self Government.
- Abolition of Indian Council.
- Separation of Judiciary & Executive.

**\[book p.172 / PDF 188\]**

- Salary of Secretary of State from British Exchequer.
- 1/2 members of the Executive Council to be Indians.
- 1/3rd representation to Muslims.

_Writings_

- _New India_ and _Common Weal_ by: _Annie Besant._
- _Al Hilal_ (a weekly Urdu-language newspaper) by: _Maulana Abul Kalam Azad_ (he also wrote a book: _India Wins Freedom_).
- _Comrade_ (a weekly English-language newspaper): _Maulana Md. Ali._

\[Box\]

- In 1906, Sri Aurobindo resigned from his post in Baroda and moved to Calcutta, where he emerged as a leader of the nationalist movement.
- He later withdrew from active politics and moved to Pondicherry in 1910, where he devoted himself to spiritual pursuits, establishing the Sri Aurobindo Ashram in 1926 and becoming a renowned philosopher and yogi.
- _"New Lamps for Old" by Shri Aurobindo Ghose, published in 1893._

**\[book p.74 / PDF 84, right column\]**

**19.III. Extremist Phase (1905-17)**

_Reasons for the Emergence of Extremists_ : 1. Realization that the true nature of British rule was exploitative 2. International influences and events which demolished the myth of White/European supremacy. These included-Abyssinia/Ethiopia's victory over Italy (1896), Boer wars (1899-1902) in which the British faced reverse, Japan's victory over Russia (1905) 3. Dissatisfaction with the achievements of Moderates 4. Reactionary policies of Curzon such as Calcutta Cooperation Act (1904), Indian Universities Act (1905) and Partition of Bengal (1905) 5. Existence of a militant school of thought and emergence of a trained leadership.

**Prominent Extremist Leaders**

1. _Bal Gangadhar Tilak_ : 'Lokmanya' Tilak was the uncompromising leader of extremists. He was influenced by Agarkar, Ranade and Naoroji. He launched two newspapers the _Kesari (in Marathi)_ and the _Maratha (in English)_. He inaugurated _Ganpati Festival (1893)_ and _Shivaji Festival (1895)_. He was deported to _Mandlay Jail (Burma)_ for writing seditious articles. He started _Home Rule League_ in 1916. He wrote _Gita Rahasya_. Tilak asserted : _'Swaraj is my Birth Right and I will have it.'_
2. _Lala Lajpat Rai_ : Extremist from Punjab. Under the influence of Arya Samaj he founded National School at Lahore. He presided over the AITUC in 1920. Boycotted Simon Commission and demonstrated against it at Lahore during which he was brutally assaulted by the police and subsequently succumbed to his injuries.
3. _Bipin Chandra Pal_ : Discarded orthodox Hinduism and entered Brahmo Samaj and visited England and America. He founded English weekly _New India_. He led the Swadeshi movement. He carried gospels of Boycott, Swadeshi, National Education, Swaraj and the Passive Resistance. He founded _Vande Matram_.
4. _Sri Aurobindo Ghosh_ : He passed ICS exam with record marks in Greek and Latin. He had a European upbringing. He worked for secret societies in Bengal and Maharashtra. He started Bengali daily _Jugantar_. He wrote seditious articles in _Vande Matram_. He was put to trial for _Maniktalla (Calcutta) Bomb Conspiracy Case_. He finally retired to the life of Yoga at Pondicherry.

_Other Extremist Leaders_ : Chakravarthi Vij\[aya\] Raghvachariar _\[right edge of the scan clips the text here\]_, Aswini Kumar Dutta, Raj Narayan Bose, T. Prakasham, Chidambram Pillai etc.

**Methods of Extremists :**

1. Passive Resistance i.e. non-cooperating with the British Government by boycotting government service, courts, schools and colleges.
2. Promotion of Swadeshi and boycott of foreign goods. _\[the list continues past the clipped bottom edge of the scan? — no further items are visible on p.74; the next visible text is p.75's heading\]_

**\[book p.75 / PDF 85, left column\]**

**The Partition of Bengal (1905) and Boycott and Swadeshi Movement (1905-08)**

- The Partition of Bengal came into effect on Oct. 16, 1905, through a Royal Proclamation, reducing the old province of Bengal in size by creating a new province of East Bengal, which later on became East Pakistan and present day Bangladesh.
- The government explained that it was done to stimulate growth of under-developed Eastern region of Bengal. But, actually, the main objective was to 'Divide and Rule' the most advanced region of the country at that time. The main reason for partition of Bengal was to destroy the political influence of the educated middle class among whom the Bengali intelligentsia were the most prominent. It also set up a communal gulf between Hindus and Muslims. The INC unanimously condemned the partition of Bengal.
- The Boycott and Swadeshi movement had its genesis in the antipartition movement which was started to oppose the British decision to divide Bengal.
- With the start of the Swadeshi movement at the turn of the contry, the Indian National Movement took a major leap forward.
- The INC took up the Swadeshi call in _Benaras Session, 1905_ presided over by _G.K. Gokhale_ and supported the Swadeshi and Boycott Movement of Bengal. Militant nationalism spearheaded by the trio of _Lal-Bal-Pal (Lala Lajpat Rai, Bal Gangadhar Tilak and Bipin Chandra Pal)_ and Aurobindo Ghosh was however, in favour of extending the movement to the rest of India and carrying it beyond the programme of just Swadeshi and Boycott of goods to full-fledged political mass struggle.
- On August 7, 1905, a resolution to boycott British goods was adopted at a meeting of the INC held in Calcutta. It was started as a purely economic measure for the development of Indian industry.
- Bonfire of foreign goods was launched on a large scale in all the major cities. _Tilak_ took the movement to different parts of India esp. to Pune and Mumbai. _Ajit Singh_ and _Lala Lajpat Rai_ spread the Swadeshi message in Punjab and other parts of Northern India. _Syed Haidar Raza_ set up the agenda in Delhi. Rawalpindi, Kangra, Jammu, Multan and Hardwar witnessed active public participation in Swadeshi Movement. _Chidambram Pillai_ took the movement to Madras Presidency which was also galvanised by _Bipin Chandra Pal's_ extensive lecture tour.

_Muslim League (1906)_ : In Dec., 1906, _All India Muslim League_ was set up by _Nawab Salimullah of Dacca (Dhaka)_ at Dacca (Dhaka). The League supported the partition of Bengal, opposed the Swadeshi movement, and demanded special safeguards for its community and a separate electorate for Muslims. This led to communal differences between Hindus and Muslims.

_Calcutta session of INC (1906)-Swaraj_ : In Dec. 1906 at Calcutta, the INC under the leadership of _Dada Bhai Naoroji_ adopted _'Swaraj'_ as the goal of Indian people. Naoroji in his presidential address declared that the goal of the INC was _'self government or Swaraj like that of United Kingdom'_.

**\[book p.75 / PDF 85, right column\]**

The differences between the moderates and the extremists, esp. regarding the pace of the movement and the techniques of the struggle to be adopted, came to head in 1907 at the Surat Session of the Congress with serious consequences for the Swadeshi movement.

_Surat Split (1907)_ : The INC split into two groups—the extremists and the moderates, at the Surat session in 1907 held on the banks of the river Tapi. The extremists were led by _Tilak, Lajpat Rai_ and _Bipin Chandra Pal_ and the moderates were led by _Gopal Krishna Gokhale_. At the Surat session, the moderate and extremist delegates of Congress met in an atmosphere surcharged with excitement and anger.

The suddenness of the Surat fiasco took the extremist leaders by surprise and they offered their cooperation to the working committee of the Congress by accepting presidentship of Ras Bihari Ghose. But the moderates would not relent as they found themselves on firm ground. The government observing the opportunity launched a massive attack on the extremists by suppressing the newspaper and arresting their main leader, _Tilak_, and sending him to _Mandalay Jail (Burma)_ for 6 years. The extremists were not able to organise an effective alternative party or to sustain the movement. _Aurobindo Ghosh_ gave up politics and left for Pondicherry. _Bipin Chandra Pal_ also left politics temporarily. _Lajpat Rai_ left for Britain. After 1908, the national movement as a whole declined.

_Morley-Minto Reforms (1909)_ : Morley-Minto Reforms were introduced in 1909 during the period when _Lord Minto_ was the Viceroy of India. The reforms envisaged a separate electorate for Muslims besides other constitutional measures. The government thereby sought to create a rift within the Congress by winning the support of the moderates on the one hand, and favour of Muslims against Hindus on the other. To achieve the latter objective, the reforms introduced the system of separate electorates under which Muslims could only vote for Muslim candidates. This was done to encourage the notion that the political, economic and cultural interests of Hindus and Muslims were separate and not common. Indian political leaders were however dissatisfied by these reforms.

_Home Rule Movement (1915-16)_ : _B.G. Tilak_ founded Indian Home Rule League at Pune on 28 April, 1916. _Annie Besant_, inspired by the Irish rebellion, started Home Rule Movement in India in September, 1916. The movement spread rapidly and branches of the Home Rule League were established all over India. B.G. Tilak wholeheartedly supported this movement. He joined forces with Annie Besant and persuaded the Muslim League to support this programme. In 1920, All India Home Rule League changed its name to _'Swarajya Sabha'_.

_Lucknow Pact–Congress-League Pact (1916)_ : An important step forward in achieving Hindu-Muslim unity was the Lucknow Pact (1916). Anti-British feelings were generated among the Muslims following a war between Britain and Turkey which opened the way for the Congress and Muslim League unity. Both the Congress and the Muslim League held session at Lucknow in 1916 and concluded the famous Lucknow Pact. The congress accepted the separate electorate and both organisations jointly demanded _'dominion status'_ for the country.

**\[Book p.76, top of left column — continues directly from the Lucknow Pact paragraph at the end of p.75\]**

Hindu-Muslim unity alarmed the British and forced the government to announce its future policy. In 1916, a British policy was announced whereby association of Indians in government increased and there was to be a gradual development of local self-governing institutions.

_Montagu Declaration / August Declaration of 1917_ : The control over the Indian government would be transferred gradually to the Indian people. This was the result of Hindu-Muslim unity exhibited in Lucknow Pact.

**PART C — Cross-book notes / discrepancies (books only — no test cross-referencing)**

**C1. Where each book's material sits**

- **Parmar** (ch.5, book pp.169–172): partition itself (Curzon, reasons, dates) → Congress sessions 1905/1906/1907 → Moderates vs Extremists (short) → Extremists' methods, Tilak, anti-partition newspapers, Abanindranath Tagore, Muslim response (League), Indian Councils Act 1909, Annulment 1911 (Delhi Durbars), long **Revolutionary Activities** timeline (1890–1915, incl. Ghadar Party, Komagata Maru, Defence of India Act), World War I, Home Rule League 1916, Lucknow Session 1916, writings box, Aurobindo box.
- **Lucent** (Part B, ch.19-III "Extremist Phase (1905-17)", book p.74 right column – p.75): reasons for emergence of extremists, four prominent extremist leaders with detail, methods, Partition of Bengal and Swadeshi/Boycott, Muslim League (1906), Calcutta session 1906, Surat Split, Morley-Minto Reforms, Home Rule Movement, Lucknow Pact. Lucent has **no** revolutionary-activities _timeline_ within the 19-III text itself (its revolutionary organisations/events are three tables on p.76 — see the Part B addendum and C6), **no** Delhi Durbar/annulment of partition, **no** World War I section, **no** Ghadar/Komagata Maru/Defence of India Act, **no** Aurobindo-Ashram/New Lamps for Old, **no** newspapers list. The Part B addendum (Montagu/August Declaration 1917 and the p.76 tables) is transcribed above.

**C2. Partition of Bengal — comparison**

- **Date of effect.** Parmar: announced July 1905, in force 16 October 1905; Lucent: "came into effect on Oct. 16, 1905, through a Royal Proclamation". **Consistent.**
- **Proposer.** Parmar: Lord Curzon (1899-1905) proposed it in 1903. Lucent: lists "Partition of Bengal (1905)" among "Reactionary policies of Curzon". Consistent.
- **Real motive.** Parmar: to "Weaken the Bengal" (nerve centre of nationalist activities); officially "administrative convenience". Lucent: 'Divide and Rule'; destroy political influence of educated middle class/Bengali intelligentsia; communal gulf; government's stated aim was to stimulate growth of the under-developed Eastern region. Consistent in substance, wording differs.
- **New province.** Parmar: East Bengal (Muslim) / West Bengal (Hindu), with a map table (Bengal 54 Mn / Muslims 16.67 %; Eastern Bengal & Assam 31 Mn / Muslims 58.06 %). Lucent: "a new province of East Bengal, which later on became East Pakistan and present day Bangladesh."
- **Curzon's other acts (⚠ dates differ).** Parmar: Calcutta Corporation Act 1899; Official Secrets Act 1904; Indian Universities Act 1904. Lucent: Calcutta Cooperation Act (1904); Indian Universities Act (1905); no Official Secrets Act. Left as printed.
- **Swadeshi/Boycott.** Both: formal Swadeshi/boycott resolution of 7 August 1905 (Parmar: "in Calcutta Townhall"; Lucent: "at a meeting of the INC held in Calcutta"). Both: INC took up the Swadeshi call at the Benaras Session 1905 presided by G.K. Gokhale. **Consistent** on date/Benaras/Gokhale; ⚠ venue wording differs (Townhall vs "meeting of the INC").
- **Regional leaders.** Parmar: Poona & Bombay — Tilak; Delhi — Sayed Haider Raza; Madras — Chidambaram Pillai; Punjab — Lajpat Rai. Lucent: Tilak (Pune, Mumbai); Ajit Singh and Lala Lajpat Rai (Punjab, Northern India); Syed Haidar Raza (Delhi); Chidambram Pillai (Madras Presidency) with Bipin Chandra Pal's lecture tour. **Consistent** (Lucent adds Ajit Singh; Parmar adds no Ajit Singh).
- **Swadeshi institutions.** Parmar only: Swadesh Bandabh Samiti (Ashwini Kumar Dutta, Barisal), Swadeshi Steam Navigation Company (V.O. Chidambaram Pillai), National Education Council, Bharat Mata painting, Rakhi/Amar Sonar Bangla, anti-partition newspapers.
- **Annulment.** Parmar only: 1911 Delhi Durbar (3rd), King George V; reunification of Bengal; capital shifted to Delhi. Lucent has none in pages read.

**C3. Congress sessions 1905–1907 and Surat Split**

- **1906 Calcutta.** Parmar: Naoroji presides; four resolutions — Swaraj, Boycott, Swadeshi, National Education (also listed in the INC chapter). Lucent: Naoroji adopts "Swaraj" as goal; presidential address quotation "self government or Swaraj like that of United Kingdom". **Consistent** on Swaraj.
- **1907 Surat.** Parmar: presided by Rash Behari Ghosh; split into Moderates and Extremists; reasons: role of INC in opposing partition; Extremists wanted mass struggle vs Moderates' faith in the British; Carrot-and-Stick model. Lucent: split on the banks of the Tapi; extremists Tilak/Lajpat Rai/Bipin Chandra Pal vs moderates Gokhale; extremists accepted presidentship of Ras Bihari Ghose; Tilak jailed at Mandalay for 6 years; Aurobindo to Pondicherry, Bipin Pal left politics temporarily, Lajpat Rai to Britain; national movement declined after 1908. **Consistent** on Surat/split/Rash Behari Ghosh (spelled "Ghosh" in Parmar, "Ghose" in Lucent).
- **Moderate vs extremist leaders.** Parmar: Moderates — SN Banerjee, KK Mitra, GK Gokhale, Pherozshah Mehta; Extremists — Lajpat Rai, Tilak, Bipin Chandra Pal, Aurobindo Ghosh. Lucent: extremist leaders — Tilak, Lajpat Rai, Bipin Chandra Pal, Aurobindo Ghosh (detailed biographies), plus "other extremist leaders" (Chakravarthi Vijaya Raghvachariar, Aswini Kumar Dutta, Raj Narayan Bose, T. Prakasham, Chidambram Pillai); its Moderate list is in the INC file's Part B.

**C4. Leaders — details compared**

- **Tilak.** Parmar: Ganpati & Shivaji festivals; "Father of Indian Unrest" (Valentine Chirol, _India Unrest_); "Lokmanya"; _Mahratta_/_Kesari_; "Freedom is my Birth Right and I Shall have it"; Mandalay Jail (Burma) **1908–1914**; wrote _Gita Rahasya_ in jail. Lucent: uncompromising extremist leader; influenced by Agarkar, Ranade, Naoroji; _Kesari_ (Marathi) and _Maratha_ (English); Ganpati Festival (**1893**), Shivaji Festival (**1895**); deported to Mandlay Jail (Burma) for seditious articles; started Home Rule League in 1916; wrote _Gita Rahasya_; 'Swaraj is my Birth Right and I will have it'. ⚠ **Festival years differ:** Parmar's Revolutionary-Activities list says "1890: Shivaji and Ganpati festivals organised by Tilak"; Lucent gives 1893 / 1895. ⚠ Lucent's Surat paragraph says "6 years" in Mandalay Jail (consistent with 1908–1914).
- **Lala Lajpat Rai, Bipin Chandra Pal, Aurobindo.** Lucent has biographical detail (Arya Samaj-influenced National School Lahore; AITUC 1920; Simon Commission assault; _New India_ weekly, _Vande Matram_; ICS/Jugantar/Manicktala/Pondicherry). Parmar has only the names in the Extremist list plus the Aurobindo box (Baroda 1906 → Calcutta; Pondicherry 1910; Ashram 1926; _New Lamps for Old_ 1893) and "Bande Matram journal was written by Bipin Chandra Pal". ⚠ Parmar's Aurobindo box says he withdrew to Pondicherry in **1910**; Lucent's Surat paragraph says he "gave up politics and left for Pondicherry" after the 1907 split without a year. No conflict; differing detail.

**C5. Muslim League, Morley-Minto, Home Rule, Lucknow — comparison**

- **Muslim League.** Parmar: 30 Dec 1906 in Dhaka by Nawab Khwaja Salimullah and Aga Khan; Muslims did not support the anti-partition movement. Lucent: Dec 1906, Nawab Salimullah of Dacca at Dacca; supported partition, opposed Swadeshi, demanded safeguards and separate electorate. **Consistent**; Parmar adds Aga Khan, exact day.
- **Indian Councils Act 1909 / Morley-Minto.** Both: Lord Minto Viceroy; separate electorate for Muslims. Parmar adds Lord Morley (Secretary of State), Minto as "Father of Communal Electorate", Satyendra Prasad Sinha as first Indian in the Viceroy's Executive Council. Lucent adds its analysis: government sought to create a rift within Congress by winning moderates and favouring Muslims; separate-electorate system explained.
- **Home Rule Movement.** Parmar: two Leagues, Tilak and Besant, both **1916**; Tilak's founded first (HQ Poona; Maharashtra excl. Bombay City, Karnataka, Central Province, Berar); Besant's (HQ Madras; Madras and Bombay City; more branches); after Tilak's death in **1920** his league renamed "Swarajya Sabha by Mahatma Gandhi". Lucent: heading "(1915-16)"; Tilak's league at Pune on **28 April 1916**; Besant's in **September 1916** (inspired by the Irish rebellion); "In 1920, All India Home Rule League changed its name to 'Swarajya Sabha'". ⚠ Consistent on 1916 and the 1920 Swarajya Sabha rename; Parmar attributes the rename to Gandhi and ties it to Tilak's death, Lucent gives no agent and does not mention Tilak's death.
- **Lucknow Pact.** Parmar: Lucknow Session 1916 presided by Ambika Charan Mazumdar; reunion of Extremists and Moderates; Lucknow Pact between INC and Muslim League; INC demands (self-government; abolition of Indian Council; separation of judiciary and executive; Secretary of State's salary from British Exchequer; half of Executive Council Indians; one-third Muslim representation). Lucent: "Anti-British feelings … following a war between Britain and Turkey"; Congress accepted separate electorate; both jointly demanded 'dominion status'. ⚠ Parmar lists specific demands but does not state that Congress accepted separate electorates or that "dominion status" was demanded; Lucent does not list the specific demands. Not contradictory — differing coverage.

**PART A — Parmar's SSC Fatman (2nd Ed., 2026): "INDIAN NATIONAL CONGRESS" (book pp.167–168, PDF 183–184)**

Chapter closes with a "■■■" end marker at the bottom-left of book p.168 (confirmed by direct read); the right half of p.168 is blank. Parmar's chapter map (PDF 170) lists this chapter's sub-headings as: Associations formed before Congress · Formation of Congress · Different Theories · Important Sessions of Congress.

**\[book p.167 / PDF 183\]**

**ASSOCIATION FORMED BEFORE CONGRESS**

- Few regional level associations were formed before Congress.

1. _Bangabhasha Prakasika Sabha_
   - _1836:_ It was started by associates of _Raja Ram Mohan Roy._
2. _British Indian Association (1851):_
   - It was founded by _Radhakanta Deb_ and _Debendranath Tagore_ in _Kolkata._
3. _East India Association_
   - _1866:_ It was founded by _Dadabhai Naoroji_ in _London._
4. _Poona Sarvajanik Sabha_
   - _1870:_ It was started by _M G Ranade_ (in Maharashtra).
5. _Indian League_
   - _1875:_ It was founded by _Sisir Kumar Ghosh_ in _Calcutta._
   - He founded Bengali newspaper: _Amrita Bazar Patrika._
6. _Indian National Association_
   - _1876:_ It was founded by _Surendranath Banerjee_ and _Anand Mohan Bose._
   - It later merged into the Congress.
7. _Madras Mahajan Sabha_
   - _1884:_ It was founded by _M. Viraraghavachari, G. Subramanian Iyer_ and _P. Ananda Charlu._
8. _Bombay Presidency Association_
   - _1885:_ It was founded by _Pherozeshah Mehta, K. T. Telang,_ and _Badruddin Tyabji._

\[Box\] **DADABHAI NAOROJI**

- He was the first Indian British member of Parliament.
- He presided three sessions of Congress (1886, 1893, 1906).
- He prepared the first estimate of the National Income of India in 1876 (he is also known as "Father of National Income in India").
- He used the term _"Swaraj"_ for the first time.
- He is also known as _"Grand Old Man of India"._
- _Rast Goftar_ (a weekly newspaper by him for Parsi community) and _Voice of India._
- Book written: _"Poverty and Un-British Rule in India"_ (explains the Drain of Wealth Theory).
- He also started a movement called _"Rahnumai Mazdayasnan Sabha"_ in 1851 along with Naoroji Furdonji and S.S. Bengalee.

**FORMATION OF CONGRESS**

- _Founder:_ It was formed by _Allan Octavian Hume_ in _1885_ known as _Father of Indian Ornithology._
- _1885:_ First meeting was called upon in _Poona_ however due to plague the venue was changed to _Bombay._
- First Session was held at _Gokuldas Tejpal Sanskrit College, Bombay_ (72 delegates attended this first Session, where only _two Muslim members and no women_ participated).
- Immediate cause for the formation of INC was:

_Ilbert Bill, 1883_

- It was a measure that allowed senior Indian magistrates to preside over cases involving British Subjects in India → But this received a huge backlash from Britishers. Due to which it had to be taken back and this exposed the Real face of Britishers to Indians.

**DIFFERENT THEORIES ON CONGRESS FORMATION**

- _Safety Valve Theory: Lala Lajpat Rai_ (monthly magazine by him: _Young India_).
- _Conspiracy Theory: R. P. Dutt._
- _Lightning Conductor Theory: Gopal Krishna Gokhale._
- \[Box\] _Dufferin was the Viceroy at the time of formation of Congress and called Congress a "Factory of Sedition"._

**IMPORTANT SESSIONS OF CONGRESS**

- _First Session:_ 1885 → Headed by _W.C. Banerjee_ in Bombay, where _72_ Delegates attended it.
- _Second Session:_ 1886 → Headed by _Dadabhai Naoroji_ in Calcutta, where _434_ Delegates attended it.
- _Third Session:_ 1887 → Headed by _Badruddin Tyabji_ in Madras. He was the _first Muslim President_ of Congress.
- _Fourth Session:_ 1888 → Headed by _George Yule_ in Allahabad. He was the _first British President_ of Congress.
- _1896:_ Our National Song, _Vande Mataram_ was sung for the first time by _Rabindranath Tagore_ (written by: _Bankim Chandra Chatterjee_).
- _1901:_ Held in Calcutta. _Mahatma Gandhi_ participated in this INC Session for the first time.
- _1905:_ Headed by _Gopal Krishna Gokhale_ in Benaras. _Swadeshi Movement_ was formally adopted here.
- _1906:_ Headed by _Dadabhai Naoroji_ in Calcutta. Four resolutions: _Swaraj, Swadeshi, Boycott,_ and _National Education_ were adopted.
- _1907:_ Headed by _Rash Behari Ghosh_ in Surat. In this Session the _Congress split into Extremists and Moderates._

**\[book p.168 / PDF 184\]**

- _1911:_ Held in Calcutta. Our National Anthem, _Jana Gana Mana_ written by _Rabindranath Tagore_ was first sung in this Session.
- _1916:_ Headed by _Ambika Charan Mazumdar_ in Lucknow leading to merger of Congress. A Joint Pact also known as _Lucknow Pact_ was signed between the Muslim League and Indian National Congress.
- _1917:_ Headed by _Annie Besant_ in Calcutta _(first Women President of Congress)._
- _1924:_ Headed by _Mahatma Gandhi_ _(only Session presided by him)_ in Belgaum (Karnataka).
- _1925:_ Headed by _Sarojini Naidu_ _(first Indian women President of Congress in Kanpur and first woman Governor of an India state (Uttar Pradesh))._
- _1929:_ Headed by _Jawaharlal Nehru_ in Lahore. The INC passed the resolution for complete independence or _Purna Swaraj_ (January 26, 1930 was officially declared to be celebrated as first _Independence Day_).
- _1931:_ Headed by _Sardar Patel_ in Karachi.
- _1937:_ Headed by _J L Nehru_ in Faizpur _(first Session to be held in a village)._

**1938-1939: Mahatma Gandhi Vs Subhash Chandra Bose**

- In _1938_ Session, held at _Haripura (Gujarat)_ and S C Bose was _elected_ as President of INC.
- In _1939_ Session, held at _Tripuri_ and S C Bose was _re-elected_ by defeating Gandhi's candidate _Pattabhi Sitaramayya,_ but later S C Bose resigned.

\[Box\]

- _Rajendra Prasad was elected as President in 1939._
- _Madan Mohan Malviya presided INC Session most number of times._
- _JB Kriplani: President during the time of Independence._
- _Youngest President of INC: Abul Kalam Azad._

\*\*Note: _It is advisable to read about the Sessions after you have read the entire Modern History._

**Indian National Congress (I.N.C.) : Bombay, 1885, A.O. Hume**

- The Indian National Union was formed in 1884 by A.O. Hume, an Englishman and a retired civil servant, in association with various national leaders who called for a conference in Pune in December 1885.
- The conference received the unanimous support of all Indian leaders, but the venue was shifted to Bombay for various reasons _(esp. outbreak of cholera in Pune)_.
- Further, the leaders decided to rename the Indian National Union as Indian National Congress.
- The first session of the Indian National Congress was held at _Gokuldas Tejpal Sanskrit College_ in Bombay under the presidentship of _W.C. Bannerji_, a veteran lawyer of Calcutta.
- It was attended by 72 delegates from all over India.
- From 1885 onwards the INC met every year and its cause spread rapidly among middle class Indians.
- With the foundation of INC in 1885, the struggle for India's independence was launched in a small, hesitant and mild but organized manner.
- The first two decade of INC are described in history as those of moderate demands and a sense of confidence in British justice and generosity. Their aim was not to be aggressive for attaining independence lest the British should suppress this. This resulted in _Indian Council Act in 1892_ which allowed some members to be indirectly elected by Indians but keeping the official majority intact.
- _Moderate Leaders_ : Dada Bhai Naoroji, A.O. Hume, Badruddin Tayebji, M.G. Ranade, W.C. Bannerji, Ferozshah Mehta, Surendra Nath Bannerji, C. Shankaran Naiyar, Madan Mohan Malviya, V.S. Shrinivas Shastri, Tej Bahadur Sapru, Gopal Krishna Gokhale, Anand Mohan Bose, E. Dinesh Wacha, Ras Bihari Ghosh, Mohan Lal Ghosh, P. Anand Charlu, C. Chintamani, R.C. Dutt, S. Subrahmanyam Aiyer, K.T. Tailang, Madhusudan Das, Rahimtulla M. Sayani.

**Select Opinions about INC**

- _'INC represents only a microscopic minority.'_ — Lord Dufferin (1884-88) _(Contemporary Viceroy)_
- _'The Congress is tottering to its fall, and one of my great ambitions, while in India, is to assist it to a peaceful demise.'_ — Lord Curzon (1899-1905) _(Viceroy)_
- _'INC is a begging institute.'_ — Aurobindo Ghosh _(Extremist Leader)_
- _'INC should distinguish between begging and claiming the rights.'_ — Bal Gangadhar Tilak _(Extremist Leader)_
- _'INC playing with bubbles.'_ — Bipin Chandra pal _(Extremist Leader)_

_(Section ends here; "19.III. Extremist Phase (1905-17)" begins immediately after in the same column — see the Bengal Partition / Extremist Phase raw extraction.)_

**PART A — Parmar's SSC Fatman (2nd Ed., 2026): "REVOLT OF 1857" (book pp.162–166, PDF 178–182)**

Chapter closes with a "■■■" end marker at the bottom of book p.166 (confirmed by direct read). Parmar's chapter map (PDF 170) lists this chapter's sub-headings as: Permanent Settlement · Ryotwari System · Mahalwari System · Sanyasi Revolt · Santhal Rebellion · 1857 Revolt · Deccan Revolt. The printed chapter is much wider than the map: it also carries three large revolt tables (Tribal / Peasant / Other) and a short "Some Other Revolts" block.

**\[book p.162 / PDF 178\]**

**PRE-1857 REVOLTS**

_Reasons for Land Revolts in India_

- Before Independence, there were three major land reforms prevailing in the country: 1. _Permanent Settlement_ 2. _Ryotwari System_ 3. _Mahalwari System_
- \[Box\] _Lord Cornwallis introduced Daroga System replacing Zamindari Thanedars._
- \[Map graphic — "LAND REVENUE SYSTEM DURING BRITISH INDIA": MAHALWARI SYSTEM → Punjab & UP; PERMANENT SYSTEM → Bengal, Bihar & Orissa; RYOTWARI SYSTEM → Bombay & Madras\]

_Permanent Settlement (1793)_

- Introduced by: _Lord Cornwallis._
- Planned by: _John Shore._
- It is also known as _"Zamindari System"._
- Under the Zamindari system, the land revenue was collected from the farmers by the intermediaries known as Zamindars (the middle man).
- _It was introduced in: Bengal, Bihar, Orissa, Uttar Pradesh, and Madhya Pradesh._
- \[Box\] _A sunset clause was introduced in the Zamindari system that required zamindars to pay afixed amount of revenue by a specific date, before sunset. If the zamindars failed to pay the revenue on time, the government would confiscate and auction off their zamindari, which was hereditary._

_Ryotwari System (1820)_

- Introduced by: _Thomas Munro_ and _Captain Alexander Read._
- It was based on _Ricardian Theory of Rent._
- Under the Ryotwari system _(Ryot: Peasant),_ the land revenue was paid by the farmers directly to the state.
- _It was introduced in: South India_ (Madras region of Tamil Nadu).

_Mahalwari System (1822)_

- Introduced by: _Holt Mackenzie_ (Concept) and later reformed by _William Bentinck_ (1833).
- _It was introduced in:_ North-Western Provinces, Central India, Punjab, and parts of Uttar Pradesh (Gangetic Valley).
- Under the Mahalwari system, the land revenue was collected from the farmers _by the village headmen on behalf of the whole village_ (and not the zamindar).
- The village or group of villages was termed a _"Mahal"_ and treated as a single revenue unit.

**REVOLTS**

_Tribal Revolts in India during British Rules_

| **Rebellion**     | **Leaders**                                  | **Year**  | **Region**                | **Key Facts**                                                                                         |
| ----------------- | -------------------------------------------- | --------- | ------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Paharia Rebellion | Raja Jagannath, Tilka Manjhi                 | 1771-1785 | Rajmahal Hills, Jharkhand | 1st tribal revolt (British adopted the policy of 'Grant-in-aid' to appease tribal chiefs).            |

**1857 REVOLT**

_Causes of 1857 Revolt — British Policies_

_1\. General Service Establishment Act (1856)_

- Passed by _Lord Canning._
- It mandated that Bengal Army sepoys must serve overseas if required (crossing the sea was considered a loss of caste).

_2\. Doctrine of Lapse_

- It was introduced by _Lord Dalhousie_ in _1848._
- If an Indian ruler died without a male heir, his kingdom would _"lapse"_ and automatically become part of the East India Company's territories.
- \[Box\]
  - _Dalhousie stated that "Awadh is like that cherry that will drop into our mouth one day"._
  - _Awadh was captured on the grounds of Misgovernance in 1856._
  - _Mirza Wajid Ali Shah was the last Nawab of Awadh._
  - _Birjis Qadr, the son of the deposed Nawab Wajid Ali Shah, was proclaimed the new Nawab of Lucknow._
  - _"Nursery of the British Army": Awadh was called so because a large number of sepoys in the British Army were recruited from this region; its annexation deeply angered them and contributed to the Revolt of 1857._
  - _The Summary Settlement (1856): Introduced by the British to bypass Taluqdars and deal directly with peasants, which dispossessed traditional landlords and pushed them to join the revolt._
- _Acquired places under Doctrine of Lapse:_ 1. Satara: 1848 2. Sambalpur, Jaitpur: 1849 3. Udaipur: 1852 4. Jhansi: 1853 5. Nagpur: 1854

_3\. Introduction of "Enfield" rifle_

- It was said that the cartridge of the rifle was wrapped in _fat of cow_ and _pig._ The cartridge had to be bitten off before loading it into the gun, thus Hindu and Muslim soldiers were reluctant to use it.
- \[Box\] • _Ishtahars:_ They glorified the co-existence of different communities under the Mughal Empire. • _Azamgarh Proclamation (1857):_ A manifesto issued by Feroz Shah (Grandson of the Mughal Emperor) appealing to all sections of society to unite against the British.
- _Mangal Pandey_ was a sepoy in the _34th Bengal Native Infantry_ at _Barrackpore_ and he refused to use the rifle.
- On _29th March 1857,_ he attacked British officers in Barrackpore.
- He was hanged on _8th April 1857._
- On _9th May 1857,_ 85 sepoys in Meerut were dismissed and sentenced to 10 years in jail for refusing to use the Enfield cartridges.
- The Revolt of 1857 began in Meerut on _10th May, 1857_ with the march towards Delhi.

_Hotspots and Leaders of 1857 Revolt_

- _Delhi: General Bakht Khan_ and _Bahadur Shah II._
- _Lucknow: Begum Hazrat Mahal._
- _Kanpur: Nana Saheb_ (adopted son of Baji Rao II)/_Tantia Tope._
- _Bihar: Kunwar Singh_ (landlord).
- _Bareilly: Bahadur Khan Rohilla._
- _Jhansi: Rani Lakshmibai_ → Original name: _Manikarnika Tambe (Manu)._

**\[book p.166 / PDF 182\]**

- _Faizabad: Maulvi Ahmadullah_ (also known as Danka Shah).
- _Uttar Pradesh: Shah Mal_ → He mobilized and organized the villagers of Pargana Baraut.

_Suppressors of Revolt_

- Delhi: _John Nicholson._
- Lucknow: _Colin Campbell._
- Kanpur: _Colin Campbell._
- Jhansi: _Hugh Rose._
- On _20th June 1858_ in _Gwalior_ → The revolt was fully suppressed.
- Bahadur Shah II was deported by the British to _Rangoon_ in Burma (presently Myanmar).
- \[Box\] _Battle of Chinhat (1857) was fought between the British forces and Indian rebels. It resulted in the defeat of British forces led by Henry Lawrence at the hands of Indian rebels led by Maulvi Ahmadullah Shah and Barkat Ahmad._

_Reasons for Failure of 1857 Revolt_

- Due to limited territorial and social base, the revolt was primarily concentrated in North India.
- Lack of coordination and leadership.
- Lack of political perspective.

_Comments_

- _V D Savarkar_ stated it as _"First war of Independence"._
- _RC Majumdar:_ It is neither "First", nor "National" nor a "War of Independence".

_Aftermath of Revolt — Government of India Act 1858_

- East India Company was abolished.
- _Queen's Proclamation_ → _Crown Rule_ (transfer of power from East India Company to the British Crown). _Queen Victoria_ was the then Queen.
- The British significantly restructured the Indian Army, and the proportion of Indian soldiers was _reduced_ (on the recommendation of the _Peel Commission_).
- Governor-General became _Viceroy_ → 1st Viceroy: _Lord Canning._

_Some Other Revolts_

_Indigo Revolt (1859)_

- It started in _Nadia_ (District) of _Bengal._
- Leaders: _Digambar Biswas_ and _Bishnucharan Biswas._
- It was against the exploitative practices of British planters who forced Indian farmers to cultivate _indigo_ instead of food crops on their land.
- It was a successful revolt.
- _Dinbandhu Mitra_ wrote _"Nil Darpan"_ (a play about Indigo Revolt). The play was translated into English by the famous Bengali poet and playwright _Michael Madhusudan Dutt._

_(right column)_

- The _Hindoo Patriot,_ an English weekly started in 1853 at Calcutta by _Girish Chandra Ghosh_ and later edited by _Harish Chandra Mukherjee,_ exposed the atrocities of British indigo planters and supported peasants during the Indigo Revolt.

_Deccan Revolt (1875)_

- It started in Maharashtra (Deccan region).
- _Cause:_ Agrarian riots against moneylenders and debt bonds.
- _Background:_ The Cotton Boom
  - Before 1861, Britain imported 3/4th of its cotton from America.
  - _American Civil War (1861):_ Supply from America stopped. Britain turned to India.
  - _Cotton Supply Association (1857):_ Formed in Britain to encourage Indian farmers to grow cotton for British industries. Farmers took loans to expand cultivation, expecting high profits.
  - → However, after the end of the American Civil War, the demand for Indian cotton declined sharply, leading to losses for farmers.
- \[Box\] _Manchester Cotton Company was formed in 1859_
- _Result:_
  - _Deccan Riots Commission (1878):_ Setup to investigate the causes.
  - The _Deccan Agriculturists' Relief Act (1879)_ was passed to protect farmers.

_Pabna Revolt (1873-1876)_

- It started in Bengal.
- Started by: _Ishan Chandra Roy_ also known as _"Bidrohi Raja"_ or _"Rebel King"._

_Ramosi Peasant Force (1879)_

- Founded by: _Vasudev Balwant Phadke_ in Maharashtra.

**PART B1 — Lucent's GK Book (2026): "17. Economic Impact of British Rule" (book p.69 right column – p.71 left column, PDF 79–81)**

Chapter begins in the lower-right of book p.69 (PDF 79), directly below the annexation table that ends ch.16; it runs across all of book p.70 (PDF 80) and the left column of book p.71 (PDF 81), where the Tribal Revolts table and Civil Revolts list appear; "18. Socio-Religious Movements in 19th-20th Centuries" begins in the right column of p.71.

**\[book p.69 / PDF 79, right column\]**

**17\. Economic Impact of British Rule**

**Three Stages of British Colonialism**

_First phase-The Mercantile phase (1757-1813)_

- The East India Company used its political power to monopolize trade and dictate terms to the traders of Bengal.
- Imposition of inflated prices of goods led to buccaneering capitalism whereby wealth flowed out of the barrel of the British trader's gun.
- Revenues of Bengal were used to finance exports to England.

_Second phase-The Industrial phase (1813-1858)_

- India was exploited as a market for British goods.
- Act of 1813 allowed one way trade for the British, as a result the Indian markets flooded with cheap and machine-made imports. Indian traders lost foreign as well as home market.
- Indians were forced to export raw materials and import finished goods.
- Heavy import duty on Indian products to England to discourage them in the market.

_Third phase-Financial phase (1860 onwards)_

- The British consolidated their position in India and made India a market for manufactures and a supplier of foodstuffs and raw materials.
- Introduction of Railways (1853), Post and Telegraph (1853), Banking System _(Avadh Commercial Bank 1881)_.

**\[book p.70 / PDF 80, left column\]**

- Heavy British investment in India and burden of public debt increases.
- Industries came into existence _(Tata Iron and Steel 1907)_.

**Drain of Wealth**

- _Dadabhai Naoroji_ cited it in his book "Poverty And Un-British Rule in India" (1901). _R.C. Dutta_ in his "Economic History of India" (Vol-I 1901, Vol-II 1903) blamed British policies for Indian economic ills.
- Drain of Wealth theory refers to a portion of national product of India which was not available for consumption to its people.
- Constituents of drain were :
  1. Extortion by company servants fortunes from rulers, zamindars, merchants and common man and sending them home.
  2. Purchasing goods out of revenues of Bengal and exporting them. This was called investment.
  3. Duty free trade provided to the British gave them a competitive edge over Indian traders. These subsidies were financed from Indian treasury.
  4. Remittances or salaries and other incomes by company officials send to England.
  5. Home charges or cost of salaries and pensions of company officials in India were paid from the treasury of India.
  6. Hefty interests were paid to British investors.
- _Effects_
  1. It stunted the growth of Indian enterprise and checked and retarded capital formation in India.
  2. It financed capitalist development in Britain.
  3. India was kept as a zone of free trade without allowing it to develop the ability to compete.
  4. Plantations, mines, jute mills, banking, shipping, export-import concerns promoted a system of interlocking capitalist firms managed by foreigners. It drained resources from India.

**Land Revenue Systems**

_Permanent Settlement/Istamarari (Sthayi) Bandobast_

- Introduced in Bengal, Bihar, Orissa and districts of Benaras and Northern districts of Madras by _Lord Cornwallis_ in 1793.
- _John Shore_ planned the Permanent Settlement.
- It declared zamindars as the owners of the land. Hence, they could keep 1/11th of the revenue collected to themselves while the British got a fixed share of 10/11th of the revenue collected. The zamindars were free to fix the rents.
- Assured of their ownership, many zamindars stayed in towns _(absentee landlordism)_ and exploited their tenants.

_Ryotwari System_

- It was introduced in Bombay and Madras in 1820. _Thomas Munro (Governor of Madras)_ and _Charles Reed_ recommended it.
- In this, a direct settlement was made between the government and the _ryot (cultivator)_.

**\[book p.70 / PDF 80, right column\]**

- The revenue was fixed for a period not exceeding 30 years, on the basis of the quality of the soil and the nature of the crop. It was based on the scientific rent theory of Ricardo.
- The position of the cultivator became more secure but the rigid system of revenue collection often forced him into the clutches of the moneylender.
- Besides, the government itself became a big zamindar and retained the right to enhance revenue it would while the cultivator was left at the mercy of its officers.

_Mahalwari System_

- Modified version of zamindari settlement introduced in the Ganges valley, NWFP, parts of Central India and Punjab.
- Revenue settlement was to be made by villages or estates with landlords. In Western Uttar Pradesh, a settlement was made with the village communities, which maintained a form of common ownership known as _Bhaichare_, or with _Mahals_, which were groups of villages.
- Revenue was periodically revised.

**Colonial Impact of Land Revenue Systems**

1. The land settlements introduced market economy and did away with customary rights. Cash payment of revenue encouraged money-lending activity.
2. It sharpened social differentiation. The rich had access to the courts to defend their property.
3. Forcible growing of commercial crops led the peasants to buy food grains at high prices and sell cash crops at low prices.
4. The stability of the Indian villages was shaken and the entire set up of the rural society began to break up.

| Koyas/Rampas         | 1922-24        | Alluri Sitaram Raju                           | British Rule                                                           |
| Naga                 | 1932           | Jadunang (1905-31) and Rani Gaidinliu later   | A reformist movement and later directed against excess of British rule |

**Civil Revolts**

- _Sanyasi (Bengal, 1780)_ : led by religious monks against British restrictions and ruin of peasantry.
- _Kattabomman Revolt (1792-98)_ : by Vira Pandya Kattabomman against imposition of British Suzerainty.
- _Velu Thampi (Travancore, 1805)_ : led by Velu Thampi against British extortions.
- _Paik Revolt (Orissa, 1817-18)_ : led by Bakshi Jagabandhu against British occupation and revenue policy.
- _Kittur Revolt (Karnataka, 1824)_ : by Chinnama and Ryappa against British interference in Kittur.
- _Pagal Panthis (Maimansinh, 1825-33)_ : by Karam Shah and Tipu. Religious nature.
- _Raju (Vizag, 1827)_ : by Birabhadra Raju.
- _Faraizi (1838)_ : by Haji Saraitullah and Dadu Mian for cause of tenants.
- _Satavandi (Maharashtra, 1839)_ : by Phond Savant and Anna Sahib against British rule.
- _Kuka (1840)_ : by Bhagat Jawahar Mal or Sian Saheb in Punjab.
- _Gadakari (1844)_ : against revenue policy in Kolhapur.
- _Poligar (Karnool, 1846)_ : by Narasimha Reddy.

**\[book p.72 / PDF 82\]**

**19\. Freedom Struggle**

**I. The Revolt of 1857**

- The Revolt of 1857 is an important landmark in the history of India which occurred during the governor-generalship of Lord Canning.

_(right column)_

- _Causes of the Revolt_ : The revolt of 1857 was a combination of political, economic, socio-religious and military causes.
  - _Political_ : Nana Sahib was refused pension, as he was the adopted son of peshwa Baji Rao II. Avadh was annexed in 1856, on charges of mal-administration. Satara, Jhansi, Nagpur and Sambhalpur were annexed owing to Doctrine of Lapse.
  - _Economic_ : Heavy taxation, forcible evictions, discriminatory tariff policy against Indian products and destruction of traditional handicrafts that hit peasants and artisans.
  - _Socio religious_ : British social reforms _(abolition of sati, 1829; legalisation of widow remarriage, 1856 etc.)_ hurt the sentiments of orthodox and conservative People.
  - _Military_ : Discrimination with Indian soldiers.
- _Immediate cause_ : The introduction of _Enfield rifles_ whose cartridges (derived from French word cartouche which hindi synonym kartus) were said to have a greased cover made of beef and pork sparked off the revolt.
- _The Beginning and Spread of the Revolt_ : On March 29, 1857, an Indian sepoy of 34 Native Infantry, _Mangal Pandey_, killed two British officers- Hugeson and Baugh on parade at Barrackpore _(near Calcutta)_. The Indian soldiers present, refused to obey orders to arrest Mangal Pandey. However, he was later on arrested, and hanged.
- The mutiny really started at _Merrut_ on 10th May 1857. The occasion was the punishment of some sepoys for their refusal to use the greased cartridges. The soldiers along with other groups of civilians, went on a rampage shouting _'Maro Firangi ko'_. They broke open jails, murdered Europeans, burnt their houses and marched to Delhi after sunset.
- The appearance of the marching soldiers next morning _(i.e. 11th May)_ in Delhi was a signal to the local soldiers, who in turn revolted, seized the city and proclaimed the 82-year old _Bahadur Shah 'Zafar'_, as _Shahenshah-i-Hindustan (i.e. Emperor of India)_.

| **Centre**         | **Beginning Date** | **Ending Date** | **Indian Leader**                                           | **British Suppressor**         |
| ------------------ | ------------------ | --------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------ |
| Delhi              | 11 May, 1857       | 20 Sep., 1857   | Bahadur Shah II 'Zafar' and Bakht Khan (Commanding General) | John Nicholson                 |
| Kanpur             | 4 June, 1857       | 6 Dec., 1857    | Nana Sahib and his loyal commander Tantiya Tope             | Colin Campbell                 |
| Lucknow            | 4 June, 1857       | 21 Mar, 1858    | Begum Hazrat Mahal                                          | Colin Campbell                 |
| Jhansi             | 4 June, 1857       | 18 June, 1858   | Rani Laxmi Bai                                              | Huge Rose                      |
| Allahabad          | 5 June, 1857       | March, 1858     | Liyaqat Ali                                                 | Colonel Neil                   |
| Jagdishpur (Bihar) | Aug., 1857         | Dec., 1858      | Kuer Singh and Amar Singh                                   | William Taylor and Vincet Eyre |

- Within a month of the capture of Delhi, the revolt spread to the different parts of India _(esp. all over the North India, Central India and Western India)_. South remained quiet and Punjab and Bengal were only marginally affected.

**\[book p.73 / PDF 83, left column\]**

**Note :**

1. _Bahadur Shah II_ : was Deported to Rangoon, where he died in 1862. His sons were dead; _Nana Sahib (original name – Dhundhu Pant)_, _Begum Hazrat Mahal_ and _Khan Bahadur Khan_ : Escaped to Nepal; _Tantiya Tope (Original name – Ramchandra Pandurang)_ : was captured and executed on 15th April, 1859; _Rani Laxmi Bai_ : Died in the battle-field; _Kuer Singh_ : was wounded and died on 26 April, 1858.
2. Sir Huge Rose described Laxmi Bai as 'the best and bravest military leader of the rebel'.
3. Other Important Leaders : Khan Bahadur Khan _(Bareilly)_, Maulavi Ahmadullah, Shah alias, Danka Shah _(Faizabad)_, Azimullah Khan _(Fatehpur)_, Devi Singh _(Mathura)_, Kadam Singh _(Merrut)_ etc.
4. English authority re-established in India during July-Dec. 1858.

_Causes of Failure_ : The Revolt of 1857 was an unsuccessful but was a heroic effort to eliminate foreign rule. The main causes were : 1. Disunity of Indians and poor organisation 2. Lack of complete nationalism-_Scindias, Holkars, Nizam_ and others actively helped the British 3. Lack of coordination between sepoys, peasants, zamindars and other classes 4. Many had different motives for participating in the revolt.

_Significance_ : The important element in the revolt lay in Hindu-Muslim unity. People exhibited patriotic sentiment without any touch of communal feelings. It no doubt began as a mutiny of soldiers, but soon turned into a revolt against the British rule in general.

**Nature of the Revolt of 1857**

- There are two main views about the nature of the Revolt of 1857 :
  1. _Sepoy Mutiny_ : Syed Ahmed Khan, Munshi Jeevan Lal and Durgadas Bandyopadhyaya _(Contemporary Historians)_; Stenley _(Secretary of state for India)_, John Lawerence, John Seeley, Malleson, R.C. Mazumdar.
  2. _National Struggle/War of Independence_ : Benjamin Disraely, Karl Marx, V.D. Savarkar, K.M. Pannikar, Ishwari Prasad, A.L. Shrivastva, Tarachand.
- _Other views_ :
  1. Racial Struggle/Black-White Struggle — _Medley_;
  2. Religious Struggle/Hindu-Muslim–Christian Struggle — _Rees_;
  3. Civilisation-Barbarism Conflict/English-Indian Conflict — _T.R. Holmes_;
  4. Hindu-Muslim Conspiracy against Christians — _Outram and Taylor_.

**Important Books on 1857**

| **Book**                                     | **Year** | **Author**       |
| -------------------------------------------- | -------- | ---------------- |
| The First Indian War of Independence 1857-59 | 1859     | Karl Marx        |
| Causes of Indian Revolt                      | 1873     | Sayed Ahmad Khan |
| The India War of Independence                | 1909     | V.D. Savarkar    |
| The Sepoy Mutiny and the rebellion of 1857   | 1957     | R.C. Mazumdar    |
| Civil Rebellion in Indian Mutinies           | 1957     | S.B. Chowdhury   |
| Rebellion, 1857 : A Symposium                | 1957     | P.C. Joshi       |
| 1857                                         | 1957     | S.N. Sen         |

**\[book p.73 / PDF 83, right column\]**

**Select Opinions on 1857**

- _"It was wholly unpatriotic and selfish Sepoy Mutiny with no native leadership and no popular support."_ — John Seeley
- _"The so-called First National War of Independence is neither 'First', nor 'National', nor 'a war of Independence'."_ — R.C. Mazumdar
- _"A national revolt rooted in deep mistrust."_ — Benjamin Disraely (Opposition Leader)
- _"The Revolt of 1857 was 'the First war of Independence.'"_ — V.D. Savarkar
- _"What began as a fight for religion ended as a war for independence."_ — S.N. Sen

**Impact of the Revolt of 1857**

1. In August 1858, the British parliament passed an act, which put an end to the rule of the Company. The control of the British government in India was transferred from the company to The British Crown.
2. A minister of the British government, called the Secretary of state for India was made responsible for the governance of India.
3. The British Governor-General of India was now also given the title of Viceroy, who was also the representative of the monarch.
4. Marked the end of British Imperialism and Princely States were assured against annexation. Doctrine of Lapse was withdrawn.
5. After the revolt, the British pursued the policy of _'divide and rule'_.
6. Far-reaching changes were made in the administration and increase of white soldiers in the army.
7. Total expense of the suppression was thrown on the Indian people.
8. The revolt had brought in its trail a difficult financial problem. The Government was burdened with a heavy debt. Two British financial exepert, James Wilson and Samuel Leing, cut down government expenditure and introduced an income tax ( ), a paper currency ( ) and annual budget ( ). With these measures the dificit was converted into a surplus by 1864. _\[the three parentheses are printed empty in the scan — years missing in the source; not filled in from memory\]_
9. It has been said that Julius Caesar dead was more powerful than Julius Caesar alive. The same may be said about the Revolt of 1857. Whatever might have been its original character, it soon became a symbol of challenge to the mighty British empire in India and remained a shining star for the rise and growth of the Indian national movement.

_(Section ends; "19. II Moderate Phase (1885-1905)" begins below in the same column — see the Indian National Congress raw extraction.)_

**C5. Revolt of 1857 — comparison**

- **Causes.** Lucent: political (Nana Sahib pension refused; Awadh annexed 1856; Satara, Jhansi, Nagpur, Sambalpur annexed by Doctrine of Lapse), economic, socio-religious (sati 1829, widow remarriage 1856), military; immediate cause Enfield rifles. Parmar: General Service Establishment Act (1856, Canning), Doctrine of Lapse (Dalhousie, 1848; Satara 1848, Sambalpur/Jaitpur 1849, Udaipur 1852, Jhansi 1853, Nagpur 1854), Awadh box, Enfield rifle. **Consistent** on Awadh 1856 and the Enfield rifle. ⚠ Lucent attributes Doctrine of Lapse annexations to Satara, Jhansi, Nagpur, Sambhalpur only; Parmar adds Udaipur and Jaitpur.
- **Start and spread.** Both: Mangal Pandey (34th Native Infantry, Barrackpore, 29 March 1857); Meerut; Delhi; Bahadur Shah II. Lucent: "Meerut on 10th May 1857"; Delhi seized 11th May (Bahadur Shah 'Zafar' proclaimed "Shahenshah-i-Hindustan", 82 years old). Parmar: 10th May; 85 sepoys dismissed 9 May; Mangal Pandey hanged 8 April. Lucent: Mangal Pandey "killed two British officers-Hugeson and Baugh".
- **Centres/leaders/suppressors.** Lucent's centre table: Delhi (Bahadur Shah II 'Zafar'/Bakht Khan; John Nicholson; 11 May–20 Sep 1857), Kanpur (Nana Sahib/Tantiya Tope; Colin Campbell; 4 June–6 Dec 1857), Lucknow (Begum Hazrat Mahal; Colin Campbell; 4 June 1857–21 Mar 1858), Jhansi (Rani Laxmi Bai; Huge Rose; 4 June 1857–18 June 1858), Allahabad (Liyaqat Ali; Colonel Neil; 5 June 1857–March 1858), Jagdishpur (Kuer Singh & Amar Singh; William Taylor & Vincet Eyre; Aug 1857–Dec 1858). Parmar's lists agree on the same names for Delhi/Lucknow/Kanpur/Jhansi and adds Bihar (Kunwar Singh), Bareilly (Bahadur Khan Rohilla), Faizabad (Maulvi Ahmadullah, "Danka Shah"), Shah Mal (Baraut), Rani Lakshmibai's original name Manikarnika Tambe (Manu). Parmar gives no start/end dates for centres, except Gwalior suppression on 20 June 1858.
- **Leaders' fates.** Lucent: Bahadur Shah II deported to Rangoon (d. 1862); Nana Sahib (Dhundhu Pant), Begum Hazrat Mahal, Khan Bahadur Khan escaped to Nepal; Tantiya Tope (Ramchandra Pandurang) executed 15 April 1859; Rani Laxmi Bai died in battle-field (26 April 1858 given for Kuer Singh); Rani "the best and bravest military leader of the rebel" (Sir Huge Rose). Parmar: Bahadur Shah II deported to Rangoon (no death date); no Nepal/Tantiya Tope execution details.
- **Failure.** Lucent: disunity/poor organisation; lack of complete nationalism (Scindias, Holkars, Nizam helped the British); lack of coordination; different motives. Parmar: limited territorial/social base (mainly North India); lack of coordination and leadership; lack of political perspective.
- **Aftermath / Act of 1858.** Lucent: August 1858 Act ended Company rule → Crown; Secretary of State for India; Governor-General also called Viceroy; Doctrine of Lapse withdrawn; Princely States assured against annexation; "divide and rule"; administrative changes and more white soldiers; James Wilson and Samuel Laing's income tax/paper currency/budget (years blank in Lucent). Parmar: "Government of India Act 1858" — East India Company abolished; Queen's Proclamation → Crown Rule; Queen Victoria; army restructured on the Peel Commission's recommendation; Governor-General became Viceroy; 1st Viceroy Lord Canning. ⚠ Lucent nowhere names the 1st Viceroy in this chapter (it says "Governor-General … Lord Canning" for the period of the Revolt). Parmar has no Queen's-Proclamation date; Lucent has no Queen's-Proclamation mention in this chapter at all.
- **Historiography.** Lucent: two views (Sepoy Mutiny — Syed Ahmed Khan, Munshi Jeevan Lal, Durgadas Bandyopadhyaya, Stanley, John Lawrence, John Seeley, Malleson, R.C. Majumdar; War of Independence — Disraeli, Karl Marx, Savarkar, K.M. Panikkar, Ishwari Prasad, A.L. Srivastava, Tarachand); other views (Medley; Rees; T.R. Holmes; Outram and Taylor); books table; five opinions. Parmar: only Savarkar ("First war of Independence") and R.C. Majumdar (neither "First", nor "National", nor "War of Independence"). **Consistent** on those two quotations.

Companion to claude/ancient-history-raw/\* and claude/medieval-history-raw/\*; format follows the Ancient History precedent (Sources → Part A/B content mirroring each book's own structure → cross-book notes), except that no "Test cross-reference index" section is included yet.

- **Lucent's GK Book (2026)**, Indian History, "18. Socio-Religious Movements in 19th-20th Centuries": book p.71 (right column) – p.72 (left column) (PDF 81–82; offset +10 in this stretch), ending where "19. Freedom Struggle / I. The Revolt of 1857" begins on the same page.
